x < 60

N

I would also like to share some of my thoughts on final grades. They are a way of quickly communicating how you did on the required assessments for this course and used as the measurement of course achievement. Your grade does not define you as a person, your worth or ability to succeed in this field, nor even necessarily how much of the course material you know or how hard you tried. I will continue to believe in you and cheer on your success regardless of what grade you get in my course. However, regardless of the reason why, if you ask me whether it is possible to change your grade, please understand that you are asking me to falsify the record of how you did on the required assessments for this course and that is something I cannot do. In a similar vein, it is not possible to do extra work to get up to the next level.

Tips for Doing Well in the Course
Embrace this opportunity: Many of us are in computer science because we find software development rewarding. This class is an invitation to become a better programmer and a better software developer. We are lucky to have this hands-on, very practical course as a part of our curriculum. This course teaches many of the things that are more important now than ever in the age of AI-augmented software development!
Keep pace with the class: This is the kind of course that relies upon practice and participation rather than book learning. For this reason, it is highly encouraged that you attend class meetings or watch the class recordings, if you miss a lecture.
Understand the nature of the class: This class is not an introductory programming class. It is a "learn by doing" class; a class that assumes you already know programming and computer science basics; and a class that involves professional skills such as group work, time management, and communication skills.
Give yourself a buffer on assignment deadlines: One common occurrence in software development is there are often unexpected delays. This class has rigid deadlines on many assessments, so stay on pace in course activities. Plan to get crucial work done ahead of time so if/when unexpected issues arise you have time to address them and still meet the deadline.
Project Details
In previous courses, you’ve learned about different programming languages, algorithms, and data structures, and you’ve written programs as part of your course assignments. But, there’s a big difference between writing a function or two to plug into a homework assignment and the type of programming that happens in the "real-world"– for example, in the type of work you may do in industry or graduate school in a few years. One of the major goals of this class is to provide a first academic experience with analyzing, designing, writing, and verifying a “big” program – one that requires working on a project that takes more than a month to complete, requires a significant number (greater than 10) separate files of software code rather than one or two, and requires documentation and other good programming practices in order to be successful. In this course, you’ll learn how to do all these things through hands-on practice.

Project topic: This semester, we will be working on a visual project involving simulation and visualization of a transportation system. You’ll start by augmenting the base simulation. Later in the semester, you will extend it to use various software patterns to configure the simulation, visualize the simulation during execution, and analyze the results. You will also use techniques to ensure that the software behaves as expected.```. This is all that is provided for the course. Do the same thing as csci 5304

# CSCI 3081W — Program Design and Development
==Full syllabus captured 2026-09-08, pasted directly from Canvas.== Closes the Program Design & Development sub-requirement of the Computer Science Core and is the major's upper-division WI course, per [[APAS]] - it does **not** close the broader Writing Intensive Lib Ed bucket on its own, one more upper-division WI course is still needed there.
## Source of Truth
`D:\Users\_Anant\10_Areas\UMN\Classes\CSCI\CSCI 3081` is the real folder for this course, still empty as of 2026-09-08 - unlike [[CSCI 5304 Board|CSCI 5304]], this syllabus came from pasted Canvas text, not a downloaded PDF. Worth saving an actual copy of the syllabus (and future slides/assignments) into that folder to keep the same source-of-truth pattern the other courses use. Desktop shortcut: `CSCI 3081.lnk` in `D:\Users\_Anant\Inbox\`.
## Catalog Info
Course number CSCI 3081W, **Program Design and Development**, Fall 2026, 4 credits. Two sections run under this syllabus - **confirm which one applies before trusting any date below**.
## Instructor & Course Staff
**Professor Liia Butler** - butle547@umn.edu - 322 Lind Hall, 207 Church St SE, Minneapolis MN 55455.
*Graduate TAs:* Sam Adeniyi (adeni026@umn.edu), Tongyu Nie (nie00035@umn.edu), Walter Sands (sands224@umn.edu).
*Undergraduate TAs:* Yassir Abdalla, Arbor Garnett, Maxwell Halbur, King Hoo, Jackson Kirchner, Raghav Mundada, Nandhini Sridhar, Karthik Sriram.
The instructor delivers lectures, sometimes assisted by undergrad TAs. Grad TAs oversee labs and help there; undergrad TAs also help in labs. Lab meetings get video demonstrations when applicable.
## Course Links
- Canvas Section 001: https://canvas.umn.edu/courses/579173
- Canvas Section 010: https://canvas.umn.edu/courses/579223
- Gradescope: https://www.gradescope.com/courses/1330310 - enrollment code **6XDWNZ**
- Piazza: https://piazza.com/class/mtga6txdip45z6
- GitHub Organization: https://github.com/CSCI3081WFa26
Canvas and Piazza are the real sources for meetings, assessments, and grades - this note is a distillation, not a replacement.
## Meetings
| Section | Lecture | Labs (Friday) |
|---|---|---|
| 001 | Tue/Thu 4:00-5:15 PM, Bruininks Hall 114 | Lab 001 F 10:10-11:00am · Lab 002 F 11:15am-12:05pm · Lab 003 F 12:20-1:10pm · Lab 004 F 2:30-3:20pm - all in Keller Hall 1-250 |
| 010 | Mon/Wed 9:45-11:00 AM, Jackson Hall 2-137 | Lab 011 F 9:05-9:55am · Lab 012 F 10:10-11:00am · Lab 013 F 11:15am-12:05pm - all in Keller Hall 1-262 |
Office hours: to be announced, starting the second week of the course.
## General Information
Theory, skills, and tools to become a good software developer - preparation for 4xxx/5xxx-level programming-intensive courses. Required course in the CS undergraduate curriculum. Topics: software development process (design, testing, maintenance), professional skills (group programming, project management, professional conduct), and writing in computer science (communicating technical information to different audiences). **Java** is the language used throughout.
## Learning Outcomes
- Understand and analyze software projects.
- Develop descriptions of software design considerations.
- Implement solutions to software design problems.
- Learn techniques to ensure software works as expected.
- Effectively use multiple genres of writing for program design and development.
*University Student Learning Outcomes:* identify, define, and solve problems - explain and describe the software development lifecycle, apply learned principles, write design documents, develop code, create tests, perform maintenance, use project/time management to coordinate group work and be held accountable for group commitments, work as a real team member toward shared goals. Students work in three-person groups across project iterations to experience the full software life cycle. The course includes quizzes, in-class activities, and participation assessments, all graded against published rubrics.
## Role of Writing
This is a writing-intensive course by design, not incidentally - assignments are reviewed for technical content, clarity, *and* quality of writing:
- **Software Documentation** - a textual intro to the codebase for new programmers (how to configure, compile, run it) plus structured API documentation via Javadoc, in both labs and the team project.
- **UML Diagrams** - visual writing in the Unified Modeling Language, used to plan development and communicate system information to other developers.
- Beyond technical writing: a **team policies and expectations document** at the start of each project iteration, **self and team feedback** after each iteration (real-world analog: workplace peer evaluations and reflections), and **code reviews** - written feedback on teammates' submitted code.
## Expected Effort
12-15 hours/week average, across reading, programming, project development, quiz study, and writing - this is stated as a large-material, writing-intensive course, not a casual one.
## Prerequisites
*Formal:* (CSCI 2021, CSCI 2041, CSci upper division) or CSci grad standing, or department consent.
*Expected skill:* prior object-oriented programming experience (Java, C++, or Python), familiarity with basic data structures (lists) and algorithms (searching).
## Course Communication
**Piazza** is the channel for all course-content communication between students, instructor, and staff (auto-added via UMN email) - check for an existing answer before posting; general questions go public (anonymous allowed), specific solutions go private. Expect a reply within 24 hours (48 over weekends). Email is for non-content matters only (e.g. arranging a conflict quiz) - a content question sent by email gets redirected to Piazza rather than answered directly.
## Optional Textbooks
No required textbook - lecture and slides are the primary material. Suggested if going deeper: *Code Complete*, *Head First Design Patterns*, *Head First Java*, *Pro Git* (also free via the U's open textbook library, Amazon, Apple Books, Google Play Books).
## Assessments Overview
Assignment list lives on Canvas; completed via Gradescope (participation and quizzes) and GitHub (labs and project iterations, also submitted through Gradescope). Check the Assignments page at the end of each class meeting.
*Participation:* synchronous attendance isn't mandatory except where flagged, but strongly encouraged. Non-mandatory classes: participation comes from an offline Gradescope assessment due **48 hours after the lecture starts** (e.g. Tuesday's lecture participation is due Thursday 4:00pm). Mandatory-attendance classes: the participation activity happens live in lecture (remote attendance can be coordinated); slides post shortly after for anyone who has to miss it. **Two lowest participation scores auto-dropped.**
*Labs:* 9 graded labs, individually submitted. Peer discussion/help is fine, but solutions must be your own - no copying or comparing solutions, and any help received (including from peers) must be cited, as a code comment or a document note (course materials don't need citing). Some lab sessions are instead used for mandatory-attendance project code-review presentations.
*Quizzes:* 6 quizzes (not counting the syllabus quiz), all proctored in **Civil Engineering Building room 230**. Lowest score auto-dropped. A 3-day window to sign up for a slot; **45-minute time limit**; no outside resources or devices (phone, smart watch, smart glasses, etc.) allowed. Discussing a quiz or its content with peers while the quiz window is open is an academic integrity violation. Roughly every other week, covering the prior two weeks' topics unless stated otherwise.
*Project Iterations:* 3 total, teams of three, using peer evaluation and code review to assess individual contribution. Code reviews happen during lab, individually - makeups exist for excused absences, and a code-review grade can be appealed to the professor for a regrade (the higher of the two scores is kept).
*Exams:* **none** - no midterm, no final exam in this course.
## Extra Credit
A team-based Team Building opportunity, one ongoing extra-credit assignment available any time in the semester, and possible additional opportunities (extra-credit questions on assignments, an announced survey) through the term.
## Grading
| Component | Weight |
|---|---|
| Syllabus Quiz | 1% |
| Participation Assignments | 8% |
| Labs | 15% |
| Quizzes | 25% |
| Project Iteration 1 (10% deliverables + 7% code review) | 17% |
| Project Iteration 2 (10% deliverables + 7% code review) | 17% |
| Project Iteration 3 (10% deliverables + 7% code review) | 17% |

*Scale (absolute - no curve, no rounding):* A 93+ (S) · A- 90-<93 (S) · B+ 87-<90 (S) · B 83-<87 (S) · B- 80-<83 (S) · C+ 77-<80 (S) · C 73-<77 (S) · C- 70-<73 (S) · D+ 67-<70 (N) · D 60-<67 (N) · F <60 (N).
> [!WARNING] The professor's own note on grade changes
> Stated directly in the syllabus: a grade is "a way of quickly communicating how you did on the required assessments," not a measure of worth or effort. Asking for a grade change after the fact is - in the instructor's own words - "asking me to falsify the record," and extra work to bump a grade up a level isn't possible. Regrade requests only work through the stated windows below, not after the fact.
## Submission & Late Work Policy
> [!WARNING] Code must compile on CSE ITLabs Linux machines - not just locally
> All coding assignments must compile and run on the **CSE ITLabs LINUX lab machines**. Code that doesn't compile there gets **zero credit**, regardless of whether it works on your own machine. Test on the actual lab environment before submitting, not just locally.
Email submissions are never accepted - only the method specified in the assignment instructions. **Late work is not accepted**, period - the syllabus gives its reasoning directly: a tight grading timeline, ample time already given, later assessments building on earlier ones, and project-iteration solutions getting released to keep everyone on pace. The built-in cushion instead: two lowest participation scores dropped, lowest lab score dropped, lowest quiz score dropped. If a deadline is missed anyway, office hours can still cover the material for feedback (not credit).
*Conflict quizzes:* arrangeable for planned absences (religious observance, UMN athletics, interviews, weddings/funerals, other important family gatherings) - contact the instructor **at least a week ahead**. For a genuine emergency (illness, injury, family emergency), contact the instructor as soon as practical instead.
## Regrade / Grade Change Policy
Participation, labs, and project-iteration-deliverable regrades: via Gradescope, within **7 calendar days** of marks posting. Quiz and project code-review regrades: by emailing the instructor, within **3 calendar days** of marks posting. Regrades on early-term work cannot be requested after final grades are out - if UMN records a marks error, that needs to reach course staff by email before the term ends, not through the regular regrade path.
## Tips For Doing Well (Instructor's Own Advice)
- This is a "learn by doing" class, not an intro course - it assumes existing programming/CS fundamentals and layers on professional skills (group work, time management, communication).
- Keep pace through practice and participation, not book study - attend or watch recordings if a lecture is missed.
- Build in a deadline buffer - the course's deadlines are rigid, and software development reliably produces unexpected delays, so finish crucial work early enough to absorb one.
- The instructor frames this course explicitly as more relevant "in the age of AI-augmented software development," not less - the skills taught are positioned as the part AI tools don't replace.
## AI / LLM Policy
No explicit AI-tool policy is stated anywhere in this syllabus text - unlike [[CSCI 5304 Board|CSCI 5304]]'s outright ban, this course doesn't mention AI or LLM use directly, beyond the one framing line above about AI-augmented development. That is **not** confirmation that AI assistance is permitted - UMN's general Student Conduct Code on scholastic dishonesty almost certainly still applies by default (plagiarism, unauthorized collaboration, etc.), it's just not spelled out course-specifically here the way CSCI 5304 does. Confirm the real policy via Canvas or Piazza before assuming anything about AI use on labs, quizzes, or project work.
## Project Details
The course's stated goal: a first real experience analyzing, designing, writing, and verifying a "big" program - more than a month of work, more than 10 separate source files, real documentation and practice, not a homework-sized function. **Project topic this semester: a visual simulation and visualization of a transportation system.** Starts by augmenting a base simulation, then extends it across the term with various software design patterns to configure the simulation, visualize it during execution, and analyze results - plus techniques to verify the software behaves as expected. This structure maps directly onto the three graded Project Iterations in the schedule below.
## Weekly Schedule (Definitive Dates)
Computed from the syllabus's own "W_ (date Start)" week labels - these are Monday-anchored, and cross-check cleanly against Section 010's actual Monday lecture day and Section 001's Tuesday/Thursday days with no internal contradiction (unlike CSCI 5304's schedule, this one held up under the same date check).

## You

There have been way too many changes across jarvis for the classes now. Starting from the root of everything - the systems laid out for writing notes: `30_Order`, everything was missing from this folder. ## Claude

This is a lot of ground to cover, and there's active parallel work happening (I can see CSCI 4041/AMES 1201/CSCI 3081 boards changing on disk as we speak). Before touching anything, let me survey what actually exists now in `30_Order` and across the course folders — the picture has clearly moved since I last looked.

I now have a clear picture. This is a genuinely large, two-part job: fix the system layer (`30_Order` standards/templates/workflows that were left as empty stubs during the reorg), then fill in real per-course content. Let me handle the quick mechanical fix myself (a consistent spelling bug across every course folder), then split the rest into two parallel background passes so neither dilutes the other.

Found the real shape of what happened: a big scaffolding pass created the whole new folder structure (`30_Order/Standards` split into `Courses/`, `Enrich/`, `Ingestion/`, `Internship/`; each course got `Weekly/`, `Textbook/`, `Preparation.md`), but left most of the actual files as empty 0-byte stubs — that's the "not done perfectly" you flagged. Concretely, I found:

- 3 of 6 course Standards blank (`Exam`, `Lab`, `Week Standard.md`), all 4 course Workflows blank, and one file (`Standards/Courses/Project Standard.md`) actually contains the *general* 20_Progress project standard, misfiled into the course folder.
- 2 templates end mid-file with a truncated stray heading (`Discussion Template.md`, `Homework Template.md`) — that's your "broken frontmatter" symptom, though the actual break is truncated content, not the YAML itself.
- Every course folder had `Preperation.md` (misspelled) at 0 bytes except CSCI 4521's, which has a few real lines already. I renamed all 7 to `Preparation.md` directly — that part's done.
- `Textbook Map.md` and `Weekly Board.md` are blank across the board too, alongside the real `Week - 1.md` notes that already exist for CSCI 4061 and CSCI 4521.

I've kicked off two background passes rather than doing this serially in front of you: one is fixing the `30_Order` infrastructure itself (the misfiled Project Standard, the blank Standards/Workflows, the truncated templates, three new templates that don't exist yet but are needed, plus the two instruction files you asked for — one in `60_Claude/07_AI_Information`, one inside `30_Order` itself, both kept short per your "lay out, don't plan" instruction). The other is filling in `Preparation.md` / `Textbook Map.md` / `Weekly Board.md` for the four courses that already have real syllabus data captured (5304, 3081, 4061, AMES 1201) — it's explicitly not touching CSCI 4521 or MGMT 3015 since neither has a real syllabus in the vault yet, so an "easy A" guide there would just be invented.

On your actual question — what would make this significantly better: the failure mode you just hit (stub files that look like coverage from a file listing but aren't) is the real risk of a scaffold-first workflow, more than any content-writing gap. The fix isn't a bigger plan, it's a cheap habit: after any bulk reorg, a dataview query surfacing 0-byte/frontmatter-only files catches this in minutes instead of a session later. The second real lever is that Standards/Templates/Workflows have to stay a strict triangle — one misfiled file (the Project Standard) silently breaks that without anything erroring. I'll fold both into the instruction files being written now rather than expand on it here.

I'll report back with the full file list once both passes finish.

- `python -c "
import datetime as dt

# weeks 1-5: parenthetical is the Sunday before that week's Tue/Thu classes
sunday_anchors = {1:'2026-09-06', 2:'2026-09-13', 3:'2026-09-20', 4:'2026-09-27', 5:'2026-10-04'}
# weeks 6-15: parenthetical IS that week's Tuesday directly
tuesday_anchors = {6:'2026-10-06', 7:'2026-10-13', 8:'2026-10-20', 9:'2026-10-27', 10:'2026-11-03',
                    11:'2026-11-10', 12:'2026-11-17', 13:'2026-11-24', 14:'2026-12-01', 15:'2026-12-08'}

def fmt(d):
    return d.strftime('%a %m/%d')

rows = []
for wk, s in sunday_anchors.items():
    sun = dt.date.fromisoformat(s)
    tue = sun + dt.timedelta(days=2)
    wed = sun + dt.timedelta(days=3)
    thu = sun + dt.timedelta(days=4)
    rows.append((wk, tue, wed, thu))
for wk, t in tuesday_anchors.items():
    tue = dt.date.fromisoformat(t)
    wed = tue + dt.timedelta(days=1)
    thu = tue + dt.timedelta(days=2)
    rows.append((wk, tue, wed, thu))

for wk, tue, wed, thu in rows:
    print(f'Week {wk:2}: Tue {fmt(tue)}  Wed {fmt(wed)}  Thu {fmt(thu)}')

print()
print('Explicit midterm date Oct 22 2026 is a', dt.date(2026,10,22).strftime('%A'))
print('Explicit final exam date Dec 22 2026 is a', dt.date(2026,12,22).strftime('%A'))
print('Real Thanksgiving Thu 2026:', dt.date(2026,11,26).strftime('%A, %b %d'))
"
`
- `python -c "
import datetime as dt
mon0 = dt.date(2026,9,7)
print('2026-09-07 is a', mon0.strftime('%A'))
for wk in range(1,16):
    monday = mon0 + dt.timedelta(days=7*(wk-1))
    tue = monday + dt.timedelta(days=1)
    wed = monday + dt.timedelta(days=2)
    thu = monday + dt.timedelta(days=3)
    fri = monday + dt.timedelta(days=4)
    sun = monday + dt.timedelta(days=6)
    print(f'W{wk:2}: Mon {monday}  Tue {tue}  Wed {wed}  Thu {thu}  Fri {fri}  (week: {monday} to {sun})')
print()
print('Thanksgiving Thu 2026-11-26 falls in week number:', (dt.date(2026,11,26)-mon0).days//7 + 1)
"
`
